by Lemma
3
.

\blacksquare

The attentive reader may have noticed that our proof technique requires the use of additive, and not multiplicative, cuts. Indeed, the use of SIH relies on the decrease of the measure
\Theta
, which is notably ensured by the upward application of any rule of the calculus.
More generally, in the proof of admissibility if the cut we initially consider has
\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
as conclusion, then we can justify a cut with conclusion
\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\chi^{\prime}
using SIH as long as we have a chain
r_{0},\dots,r_{n}
of application of rules of
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
of the following form.

\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi\lx@proof@logical@and\ldots\vdots\lx@proof@logical@and\ldots\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\chi^{\prime}\ldots\ldots

However, the contraction rule does not ensure the decrease of the measure
\Theta
from conclusion to premise: it is not the case that
\Theta(\Gamma,\varphi,\varphi\Rightarrow\chi)\slex\Theta(\Gamma,\varphi\Rightarrow\chi)
. So, this prevents us from allowing one of
r_{0},\dots,r_{n}
above to be
(\text{Ctr})
. This is where multiplicative cuts are problematic: they most often use the contraction rule as follows, where
\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
is the conclusion of the initial cut and
\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow\chi^{\prime}
is the conclusion of the cut we want to justify through SIH.

\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\varphi

\varphi,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow\chi^{\prime}

SIH

\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow\chi^{\prime}

\vdots

\scriptstyle(\text{Ctr})^{*}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi

Unfortunately, the presence of the contraction rule above
\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
disallows us from using SIH on
\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow\chi^{\prime}
, as we are not ensured that the measure decreased between the two sequents. So, our proof technique prohibited us from using multiplicative cuts, forcing us to use additive ones. This observation was already made by Goré and Shillito
[
28
]
.

Using our purely syntactic proof of cut-admissibility above, we easily obtain a cut-elimination procedure for the calculus
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
extended with (
cut
), by simply repetitively eliminating topmost cuts first. To effectively prove this statement in Coq we explicitly encode the additive cut rule as follows:

(
\Gamma
0++
\Gamma
1

*\

\varphi
)

(
\Gamma
0++
\varphi
::
\Gamma
1

*\

\chi
)

(
\Gamma
0++
\Gamma
1

*\

\chi
)

We encode the calculus
\mathsf{G4iSLt}+(\text{cut})
as
G4iSLt_cut_rules
, i.e.
G4iSLt_rules
enhanced with (
cut
).
Finally, we turn to the elimination of additive cuts:

Theorem 5.2

The additive cut rule is eliminable from
\mathsf{G4iSLt}+(\text{cut})
.

⬇

Theorem

G4iSLt_cut_elimination

:

forall

s
,

(
G4iSLt_cut_prv

s
)

->

(
G4iSLt_prv

s
).

The above theorem shows that any proof in
\mathsf{G4iSLt}+(\text{cut})
of a sequent, i.e.
G4iSLt_cut_prv

s
, can be transformed into a proof in
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
of the same sequent.
As this theorem is in fact a constructive function based on
Type
, we can use the extraction feature of Coq and obtain a cut-eliminating Haskell program.

6
Conclusion

This paper introduces a sequent calculus for
\mathsf{iSL}
, denoted
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
. It is an improvement over the sequent calculus
\mathsf{G4iSL}
from
[
23
]
, because backward proof search for
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
is strongly terminating (instead of weakly terminating) shown via a new well-founded measure, and cut-elimination is proved directly (instead of indirectly via an equivalent calculus based on
\mathsf{G3i}

[
23
]
). All our results are formalised in Coq in a constructive way. In turn, Coq’s extraction mechanism can generate a Haskell program for the cut-elimination procedure for
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
.

One of the reasons to develop
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
is to use its strongly terminating proof search to investigate uniform interpolation, a strengthening of Craig interpolation, in the setting of intuitionistic provability logics.
Typically, calculi with good (weakly or strongly) terminating proof search form good grounds for constructive proofs of uniform interpolation (see e.g.
[
40
,
8
,
30
,
5
,
3
,
24
,
2
,
4
]
).

We also suggest to develop a countermodel construction for
\mathsf{G4iSLt}
similarly to the one for
\mathsf{G4iSL}
in
[
23
]
. Furthermore, as
\mathsf{iSL}
is an intuitionistic modal logic only defined with

, there is the question how it can be extended by

operators. It is clear from the literature of intuitionistic modal logics that several choices can be made (e.g.
[
18
,
43
,
7
,
35
,
47
]
), so we leave this for future work.

Proof
(of Theorem
5.1
)

(I)

\mathbb{r_{1}=}\textbf{(\text{IdP})}:
then we have that
\varphi=p
. Consequently,
\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
is of the form
\Gamma_{0},p\Rightarrow\chi
. Also, the conclusion of
r_{2}
is of the form
\Gamma_{0},p,p\Rightarrow\chi
. We can apply the contraction rule (
Ctr
) to obtain a proof of
\Gamma_{0},p\Rightarrow\chi
.

(II)

\mathbb{r_{1}=}\textbf{(\text{$\bot$L})}
: Then
r_{1}
must have the following form where
\Gamma_{0},\bot=\Gamma
:

\scriptstyle(\text{$\bot$L})

\Gamma_{0},\bot\Rightarrow\varphi

Thus, we have that the sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
is of the form
\Gamma_{0},\bot\Rightarrow\chi
, and is an instance of (
\bot
L
). So we are done.

(III)

\mathbb{r_{1}\in}\{\textbf{(\text{$\land$L}), (\text{$\lor$L}), (\text{$p\!\rightarrow$L}), (\text{$\land\!\rightarrow$L}), (\text{$\lor\!\rightarrow$L})}\}
: In all these cases, the cut formula is not principal in
r_{1}
so it is preserved in the premise. Given that the rules considered are invertible, we simply take the conclusion of
r_{2}
and use the corresponding invertibility lemma to destruct the principal formula of
r_{1}
. Then, we use SIH to cut on
\varphi
in the obtained premises, and apply
r_{1}
on the conclusion of the cut. As an example, we consider the case of (
\land
L
), where
r_{1}
is of the following form and where
\Gamma_{0},\psi\land\delta=\Gamma
:

\Gamma_{0},\psi,\delta\Rightarrow\varphi

\scriptstyle(\text{$\land$L})

\Gamma_{0},\psi\land\delta\Rightarrow\varphi

Thus, we have that the sequents
\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
and
\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
are respectively of the form
\Gamma_{0},\psi\land\delta\Rightarrow\chi
and
\varphi,\Gamma_{0},\psi\land\delta\Rightarrow\chi
. Using the invertibility of (
\land
L
), proven in Lemma
2
, on
\varphi,\Gamma_{0},\psi\land\delta\Rightarrow\chi
we obtain a proof of the sequent
\varphi,\Gamma_{0},\psi,\delta\Rightarrow\chi
. Then, we proceed as follows.

\Gamma_{0},\psi,\delta\Rightarrow\varphi

\varphi,\Gamma_{0},\psi,\delta\Rightarrow\chi

SIH

\Gamma_{0},\psi,\delta\Rightarrow\chi

\scriptstyle(\text{$\land$L})

\Gamma_{0},\psi\land\delta\Rightarrow\chi

Note that the use of SIH is justified here as the last rule in this proof entails

\Theta(\Gamma_{0},\psi,\delta\Rightarrow\chi)\slex\Theta(\Gamma_{0},\psi\land\delta\Rightarrow\chi)
by Lemma
3
.

(IV)

\mathbb{r_{1}\in}\{\textbf{(\text{$\land$R}), (\text{$\lor$R${}_{1}$}), (\text{$\lor$R${}_{2}$})}\}
: In all these cases, the cut formula is principal in
r_{1}
so it is deconstructed in the premise. Given that the corresponding left rules are invertible, we simply take the conclusion of
r_{2}
and use the adequate invertibility lemma to destruct the cut formula. Then, we use PIH to cut on the obtained subformulae. As an example, we consider the case of (
\lor
R
1
), where
r_{1}
is of the following form and where
\psi\lor\delta=\varphi
:

\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi

\scriptstyle(\text{$\lor$R${}_{1}$})

\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi\lor\delta

Thus, we have that the sequents
\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
is of the form
\psi\lor\delta,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
. Using the invertibility of (
\lor
L
), proven in Lemma
2
, on
\psi\lor\delta,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
we obtain proofs of the sequents
\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
and
\delta,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
. Then, we proceed as follows.

\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi

PIH

\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi

(V-a)
If
r_{2}
is (
\rightarrow
R
) then it must have the following form where
\chi_{0}\rightarrow\chi_{1}=\chi
:

\scriptstyle(\text{$\rightarrow$R})

\varphi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{1},\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi_{0}\rightarrow\chi_{1}

We can use the weakening rule (
Wkn
) on the
proof of
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{1}

to get a proof of
\chi_{0},\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{1}

of no greater height. Proceed as follows.

\chi_{0},\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{1}

SIH

\chi_{0},\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi_{1}

\scriptstyle(\text{$\rightarrow$R})

\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi_{0}\rightarrow\chi_{1}

Note the use of the additive cut to contract on
\chi_{0}
.
The use of SIH is justified here as the last rule in this proof gives us
\Theta(\chi_{0},\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi_{1})\slex\Theta(\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi_{0}\rightarrow\chi_{1})

.

\varphi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{1},\gamma_{0}\rightarrow(\gamma_{1}\rightarrow\gamma_{2}),\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi

\gamma_{0}\rightarrow(\gamma_{1}\rightarrow\gamma_{2}),\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\!\rightarrow
L

(VI)

\mathbb{r_{1}=}\textbf{(\text{$\rightarrow\rightarrow$L})}
: Then
r_{1}
is as follows, where
(\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
.

\gamma_{1}\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1}

\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\varphi

\scriptstyle(\text{$\rightarrow\rightarrow$L})

(\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\varphi

Thus, we have that the sequents
\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
and
\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi
are respectively of the form
(\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi
and
\varphi,(\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi
. Using the admissible rule (
\rightarrow\rightarrow
LIR
) on
\varphi,(\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi
we obtain a proof of the sequent
\varphi,\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi
. Then, we proceed as follows.

\gamma_{1}\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1}

\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\varphi

\varphi,\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi

SIH

\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi

\scriptstyle(\text{$\rightarrow\rightarrow$L})

(\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi

Note that the use of SIH is justified as the last rule in this proof gives us
\Theta(\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi)\slex\Theta((\gamma_{0}\rightarrow\gamma_{1})\rightarrow\gamma_{2},\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow\chi)
by Lemma
3
.
